Abstract written by a model

In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I: declare the pure, unchangeable, eternal law [universally] The Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future declare that all breathing, existing, living, sentient creatures should not be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I: declare truth [beyond doubt] The Ginas have declared the truth beyond doubt. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Mahâvîra is the Revered One who teaches renunciation, nonviolence, careful eating, and strenuous asceticism while enduring hardship. (Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I) In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I: raise discussion [in this world] Some Sramanas raise the discussion that living beings may be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away without wrong. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I, the houseless is a desireless religious wanderer who renounces possessions, avoids harm, practices penance, and seeks Nirvâna. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I: Nirgrantha is a person equanimous toward heat, cold, pleasure, and pain, so unattached that he does not feel penance’s austerity. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra): A fellow-ascetic is an agreeable, unshunned companion governed by rules of conduct. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra): The Kevalin is an omniscient authority and teacher who gives reasons for religious restrictions.

and 47 more works

In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Nirgrantha is a controlled mendicant who scrutinizes alms, avoids improper entertainments and food, and distributes provisions under spiritual authority. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Sramanas are houseless mendicants who renounce property and sensory pleasures while following strict rules for alms, travel, lodging, and religious practice. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra): Gina, an infinite knowing one who illumines three worlds, is the source of commandments guiding mendicants to exert themselves and respect one another, and proclaimed great vows throughout the earth. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), the monk follows detailed rules governing speech, possessions, movement, study, alms, and bodily conduct. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra): teach four kinds of speech [past, present, and future Arhats] All past, present, and future Arhats have taught and declared, teach and declare, and will teach and declare the four kinds of speech. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra is the omniscient Arhat who renounces worldly life, attains Kevala, and teaches the vows and classes of living beings. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), the monk conducts disciplined begging-tours and strictly avoids improper food, companions, conduct, and desire. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Nirgranthas are houseless practitioners instructed by Mahâvîra to observe the great vows, careful conduct, restraint, and nonattachment. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), the mendicant is a self-controlled sage who renounces attachments, endures suffering without harming beings, and gains wisdom through austerity. In Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), the monk follows shared rules for begging, lodging, travel, study, nonattachment, and avoiding harm to living beings. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I, Mahâvîra is the omniscient Arhat and supreme teacher who renounces all ties, conquers Karman, and teaches the highest Law. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: Arhat, a wise person, pondered on the Law and proclaimed its teaching on carefulness. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: A Leader, Saviour, and Venerable One who knows past, present, and future, removes obstacles to right faith, and incomparably knows and expounds the Law. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: Sramanas are religious ascetics, not monks who seek houses with fine food. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: The Great Hero is a being without Karman who understands Karman, is not born again or dies, annihilates former works, and creates no new works. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I, a Nirgrantha is a disciplined ascetic who knows the true Self, observes equanimity, renounces sin, and seeks liberation. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: Kâsyapa is an illustrious, omniscient religious teacher associated with and proclaiming a Law practiced through abstinence, control, hardship, and care for sick brothers. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I, Sramanas are houseless ascetics who beg lawfully, practice austerities and nonviolence, endure calamities, and seek final liberation. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: proclaim Law [well] The Arhats have well proclaimed the commandments that practitioners should follow. In Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I: The Ginas, past and future, commend virtuous conduct under the Law of Kâsyapa. In Sutrakritanga, Book II: Followers of the Sramanas understand living and nonliving things, doctrine including Âsravas and Samvara, virtues, sins, Karman, bondage, and liberation. In Sutrakritanga, Book II: Nirgrantha Sramanas are a group with a creed accepted by their followers, who provide food, clothing, medicines, and necessities. In Sutrakritanga, Book II: teach opinions The Ginas teach the opinions to which the monk should conform himself. In Sutrakritanga, Book II, Mahâvîra is a teacher who explains doctrine, Karman, living beings, renunciation, and the vow leading to liberation while guiding monks. In Sutrakritanga, Book II, Sramanas are disciplined practitioners who teach the Law, observe vows and nonviolence, avoid harmful food and conduct, and are contrasted with violent practitioners. In Sutrakritanga, Book II: The Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future declare that all sorts of living beings should not be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: Arhat Gñâtriputra, venerable native of Vaisâlî, speaks Sixth Lecture teachings and possesses the highest knowledge and faith. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: are_like beasts [when ignorant of the prohibition of killing living beings] Some who call themselves Sramanas are like ignorant beasts because they do not know the prohibition of killing living beings. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra, the monk is a disciplined wandering mendicant who practices self-control, austerities, study, and the Law to attain liberation. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra, Nirgrantha is a renunciant monk who practices vows, austerities, meditation, solitary chastity, and freedom from sensual attachment. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: Mahâvîra is a Venerable ascetic of Kâsyapa Gôtra and a teacher. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: A Nirgrantha enters an order, learns Law, receives discipline, and attains difficult Bôdhi, but may afterward live as he likes and become a bad Sramana. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: A Sramana repeatedly suppresses delusion and controls himself, knowing and renouncing women; though roughly affected by externals, he must not hate them inwardly. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: The Ginas are enlightened authorities who proclaim an eternal Law, teaching souls, sin’s consequences, and righteousness; Sañgaya and many kings adopt their faith. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: Vardhamâna, a venerable Gina and Prophet of the Law, is world-known; through Gautama, his Law mandates five vows and monastic nudity. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: The Nirgranthas follow doctrines centered on great vows, penance, self-control, and correct behavior; careless or sensual followers fail, while strict practice frees them from sinful influences and crosses Samsâra. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra, Sramana is a restrained, celibate, houseless ascetic who practices vows, austerities, meditation, and renunciation to attain liberation. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: The Ginas are teachers with the highest road, abandoned by self-willed sinners pursuing pleasure. In Uttaradhyayana Sutra: Mahâvîra, a noble venerable Ascetic of the Kasyapa Gôtra and a disciple’s teacher, delivered The Exertion in Righteousness. In Kalpa Sutra, Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra is the last Tîrthakara who renounces worldly life, attains omniscience, establishes the monastic community, and dies liberated. In Kalpa Sutra: are conquerors who have conquered fear The Ginas are praised as conquerors who have conquered fear, reached the path of perfection, and are liberated and liberators. In Kalpa Sutra: Vardhamâna, a Gñâtri Kshatriya and prince of Videha, was named by his parents after their wealth and possessions increased following his birth. In Kalpa Sutra, Sramanas are ascetic communities following Mahâvîra and earlier Tîrthakaras, organized under named leaders and possessing specified attainments. In Kalpa Sutra: Sramanas are an excellent community of eighteen thousand led by Varadatta, within Arishtanemi’s wider community of sages and disciples. In Kalpa Sutra: has_death_after Sîtala [by a krore of Sâgaropamas less 42,003 years and eight and a half months] Vîra’s death is used as the reference point for Sîtala’s death. In Kalpa Sutra: The Nirgrantha Sramanas are present spiritual descendants of Ârya-Sudharman; following earlier teachers and the Sthaviras, they commence Paggusan after a month and twenty rainy-season nights. In Kalpa Sutra: The Arhats are revered in opening obeisances and Sakra’s praise; across past, present, and future, they are born or moved at conception into high, noble, royal, or pure families.

  • monk does not give food (immediately or mediately to a heretic or householder on a begging-tour)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour should not give, immediately or mediately, food, &c., to a heretic or a householder”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book II, translated by Hermann Jacobi / Second Book / First Part. / First Lecture, Call
  • Sramanas are recipients of food (food prepared by a householder for many persons)
    • “prepared by the householder for the sake of many Sramanas and Brâhmanas, guests, paupers, and beggars”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book II, translated by Hermann Jacobi / Second Book / First Part. / First Lecture, Call
  • monk does not accept food (when many people are entertained from vessels, pots, baskets, or heaps of food prepared by the giver)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour should not accept food, &c., in the following case: when, on the eighth or paushadha day, on the beginning of a fortnight, of a month, of two, three, four, five, or six months, or on the days of the seasons, of the junction of the seasons, of ...”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Second Lesson.
  • monk accepts food (from unblamed, uncensured families)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour may accept food, &c., from unblamed, uncensured families, to wit, noble families, distinguished families, royal families, families belonging to the line of Ikshvâku, of Hari, cowherds' families, Vaisya families, barbers' families, carpenters' ...”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Second Lesson.
  • Sramanas should not accept food (on a begging-tour when its acceptability is doubtful)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour should not accept such food, &c,, about the acceptability or unacceptability of which his (or her) mind has some doubts or misgivings for such food, &c.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Third Lesson.
  • food is given for Sramanas (for the sake of all of them)
    • “this food, &c., has been given for the sake of all of you; eat it or divide it among you.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Fifth Lesson.
  • Nirgrantha shares food (with other beggars)
    • “Knowing this, he should join the other beggars, and after consideration say unto them: 'O long-lived Sramanas! this food, &c., is given for the sake of all of you; eat it or divide it among you.'”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Fifth Lesson.
  • Nirgrantha should not accept food (taken from a granary or vault by contorting the layman up and down and horizontally)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour should not accept food, &c., which a layman, for the sake of the mendicant, has taken from a granary or vault by contorting himself up and down and horizontally;”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Seventh Lesson.
  • monk should not smell food (when smelling it in travellers' houses, garden houses, householders' houses, or maths)
    • “When a monk or a nun on a begging-tour smells, in travellers' houses or garden houses or householders' houses or maths, the scent of food or drink or sweet scents, they should not smell them, being indifferent against smell, and not eager or desirous or greedy or covetous of the ...”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Eighth Lesson.
  • monk does not select food (by smell; everything received is to be consumed)
    • “they should consume everything, whether it be sweet smelling or bad smelling, and reject nothing.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Ninth Lesson.
  • food may be accepted monk (when put before the door for another and permitted or given by the householder)
    • “A monk or a nun on a begging-tour should not accept food, &c., which for the sake of another has been put before the door, if the householder has not permitted him to do so, or he gives it him”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Ninth Lesson.
  • Nirgrantha should not cover food (with distasteful food to conceal it from others)
    • “A single mendicant, having collected agreeable food, might cover it with distasteful food, thinking: 'The teacher or sub-teacher, &c., seeing what I have received, might take it himself; indeed, I shall not give anything to anybody!' As this would be sinful, he should not do so.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Tenth Lesson.
  • Nirgrantha should not eat only good food (leaving discoloured and tasteless food)
    • “A single mendicant, having received some food, might eat what is good, and bring what is discoloured and tasteless; as this would be sinful, he should not do so.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Tenth Lesson.
  • Nirgranthas inspects food (before eating and drinking)
    • “A Nirgrantha eats and drinks after inspecting his food and drink; he does not eat and drink without inspecting his food and drink.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra endowed with the highest knowledge and intuition
  • Nirgranthas consumes food (with permission of the superior)
    • “A Nirgrantha consumes his food and drink with permission (of his superior), not without his permission.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra endowed with the highest knowledge and intuition
  • Nirgranthas does not consume excessively food (does not drink liquors or eat highly-seasoned dishes)

    • “He should know what alms may be accepted, and should strictly keep these rules”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Eighth Lecture.
  • Nirgrantha does not take food and drink (from householders unless they give it themselves)
    • “A Nirgrantha is forbidden to take from householders, if they do not give it themselves, bed, lodging, drink, food, or any dainties and spices;”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Fifteenth Lecture.

colourtopiccauses pain, causes ruin, is indifferent to3 facts · 1 work
  • colour causes ruin Sramana (when passionately desired)
    • “He who is passionately fond of 'colours,' will come to untimely ruin; just as an impassioned moth which is attracted by the light rushes into death. (24)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.
  • colour causes pain Sramana (when passionately hated)
    • “He who passionately hates (a colour), will at the same moment suffer pain. It is the fault of an undisciplined man that he is annoyed (by a colour); it is not the 'colour' itself that annoys him. (25)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.
  • colour is indifferent to Sramana (when dispassionate)
    • “But a man who is indifferent to 'colours,' is free from sorrows; though still in the Samsâra, he is not affected by that long succession of pains, just as the leaf of the Lotus (is not moistened) by water. (34)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.
food and drinkobjectdoes not cook, eats, rejects3 facts · 1 work
  • Sramana does not cook food and drink (nor cause another to cook; out of compassion for living beings)
    • “The same holds good with the cooking of food and drink, or with one's causing them to be cooked. Out of compassion for living beings one should not cook nor cause another to cook. (10)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Fifth Lecture.
  • Sramana eats food and drink (for sustenance of life, not for pleasant taste)
    • “A great sage should not eat for the sake of the pleasant taste (of the food) but for the sustenance of life, being not dainty nor eager for good fare, restraining his tongue, and being without cupidity. (17)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Fifth Lecture.
  • Sramana rejects food and drink (when the time of death arrives)
    • “Rejecting food when the time of his death arrives, and leaving the human body, he becomes his own master, and is liberated from misery. (20)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Fifth Lecture.

womengroupare difficult for, avoids, does not watch3 facts · 1 work
  • Sramana avoids women (as a chaste monk)
    • “As it is not safe for mice to live near the dwelling of a cat, so a chaste (monk) cannot stay in a house inhabited by women. (13)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.

    • “A Sramana, engaged in penance, should not allow himself to watch the shape, beauty, coquetry, laughter, prattle, gestures, and glances of women, nor retain a recollection of them in his mind. (14)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.
  • women are difficult for Sramana (who longs for liberation)
    • “To a man who longs for liberation, who is afraid of the Samsâra, and lives according to the Law, nothing in the world offers so many difficulties as women who delight the mind of the ignorant. (17)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Second Lecture.

houseobjectdoes not build, does not long for2 facts · 1 work

    • “Even in his thoughts a monk should not long for a pleasant painted house filled with the fragrance of garlands and frankincense, secured by doors, and decorated with a white ceiling-cloth. (4)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Thirty-Fifth Lecture.

    • “Know this: those who use cold water, eat seeds, accept things especially prepared for them, and have intercourse with women, are (no better than) householders, but they are no Sramanas.”Sutrakritanga, Book II · Sixth Lecture, Called .

    • “If those who eat seeds, use (cold) water, and have intercourse with women, are admitted to be Sramanas, then householders too are Sramanas; for they do the same things.”Sutrakritanga, Book II · Sixth Lecture, Called .

Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future first appears

Abstract written by a model

declare the pure, unchangeable, eternal law [universally] The Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future declare that all breathing, existing, living, sentient creatures should not be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fourth Lecture, Called Righteousness. / First Lesson. .. Fourth Lesson.

The Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future declare that all breathing, existing, living, sentient creatures should not be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away.

Ginas in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Ginas first appears

Abstract written by a model

declare truth [beyond doubt] The Ginas have declared the truth beyond doubt. Their teaching is used to guide reflection, break the continuity of sins, and direct the person toward liberation.

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fifth Lecture, Called Essence of the World. / First Lesson. .. Sixth Lesson.

The Ginas have declared the truth beyond doubt. Their teaching is used to guide reflection, break the continuity of sins, and direct the person toward liberation.

Mahâvîra in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mahâvîra is the Revered One, a Tîrthakara, wise Brahman, ascetic, and sage whose discourse Sudharman heard. He teaches comprehension and renunciation of sin’s causes, faith, the three vows, and a law for the instructed to follow. As a naked, houseless, world-relinquishing sage, he practices strenuous asceticism, nonviolence, careful eating, and avoidance of karman and sin. After one year and one month he gives up his robe, then wanders for thirteen years in meditation and severe self-control. Lodging in resting-places, he carefully begs, avoids medical treatment and shelter, and endures dogs’ attacks, hunger, thirst, heat, cold, and bodily pain without desire or disturbance, proceeding toward Nirvâna.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I / First Book. / First Lecture, Called Knowledge of the Weapon. / First Lesson. .. Seventh Lesson.

Mahâvîra is the Revered One whose discourse Sudharman heard. He teaches the comprehension and renunciation of the causes of sin and the faith to be coveted.

Seventh Lecture, Called Liberation. / First Lesson. .. Eighth Lesson.

Mahâvîra is identified with the wise Brahman who has enjoined three vows. His teaching is presented as the law that the instructed should thoroughly follow.

Eighth Lecture, (Called) the Pillow of Righteousness. / First Lesson. .. Fourth Lesson.

Mahâvîra, the Venerable One and great Hero, wandered for thirteen years in meditation and severe self-control. He gave up his robe, endured attacks, hunger, thirst, heat, cold, and bodily pain, avoided medical treatment, begged carefully, and proceeded undisturbed toward Nirvâna.

houseless in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where houseless first appears

Abstract written by a model

Houseless is a desireless religious person who gives up the world, ceases to act, renounces desire, and wanders toward final liberation. They avoid unclean possessions and transactions, possess only code-permitted food, prescribed clothing, an alms-bowl, and examined straw for secluded rest. They do no harm to living beings, reject goods gained through killing, theft, force, or injury, restrain senses and bodily motion, and avoid sin through disciplined conduct. They speak little, meditate, endure pain and cold patiently, even rejecting fire, and practice penance and nonviolence. Following vows and limiting possessions, they continue contemplation, endure religious death without attachment to life or death, and seek Nirvâna and freedom from bonds.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lecture, Called Conquest of the World. / First Lesson. .. Sixth Lesson.

The houseless person gives up the world, ceases to act, has no wishes caused by discernment, and avoids unclean possessions and transactions. He wanders freely from uncleanliness and proceeds toward final liberation.

Seventh Lecture, Called Liberation. / First Lesson. .. Eighth Lesson.

Mendicants should not accept food, clothing, lodging, or other goods obtained through killing, theft, force, or injury to living beings. They should limit their possessions, follow their vows, restrain their senses and bodily motions, endure hardship, and continue contemplation toward religious death and liberation.

Eighth Lecture, (Called) the Pillow of Righteousness. / First Lesson. .. Fourth Lesson.

Monks are instructed to follow the rule of the Venerable One by living with restraint, speaking little, meditating, enduring hardship, and avoiding harm to living beings. Some monks sought shelter and more clothing in the cold, while the Venerable One endured the cold without such protection.

fellow-ascetic in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where fellow-ascetic first appears

Abstract written by a model

A fellow-ascetic is an agreeable, unshunned companion governed by rules of conduct. Nearby fellow-ascetics who follow the same rules may receive food or drink an inadvertently accepting mendicant cannot consume. A zealous brother following the same rules may be invited to collected food; those following other rules may be offered begged furnishings, but not something possessed for somebody else.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Tenth Lesson.

Fellow-ascetics may receive food or drink that an inadvertently accepting mendicant cannot consume, provided they are nearby, follow the same rules of conduct, are agreeable, and are not to be shunned.

Seventh Lecture, Called Regulation of Possession. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

A fellow-ascetic who follows the same rules and is a zealous brother may be invited to food collected by a mendicant. Fellow-ascetics following other rules may be offered a footstool, bench, bed, or couch begged by the mendicant, but not something possessed for somebody else.

Kevalin in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Kevalin first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Kevalin is an omniscient authority and teacher who gives reasons for religious restrictions. He explains why to reject food prepared over fire, inspect robes for hidden things or living matter, and judge lodging fitness. Across the lessons, he warns that food at entertainments may involve sinful preparation or acquisition; entertainments with feasts risk illness, sensual temptation, and sin; and food from elevated places, broken earthenware, or disturbed fire can harm living beings and incur sin.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lesson.

The Kevalin assigns as the reason for avoiding food given at such entertainments that accepting it may incur the sin of using food prepared for the monk, mixed with living beings, bought, stolen, or forcibly taken.

Third Lesson.

The Kevalin explains that attending entertainments preceded or followed by feasts may expose a mendicant to illness, sensual temptation, and sin. He also explains the dangers of much-frequented and vulgar entertainments.

Seventh Lesson.

The Kevalin explains that accepting food from elevated places may lead a layman to fall and injure or kill living beings. The Kevalin also explains that breaking earthenware or stirring and brightening fire can injure living beings and cause the pakkhâkamma sin.

Nirgrantha in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha first appears

Abstract written by a model

Nirgrantha is a well-controlled mendicant, described through rules governing conduct on a begging-tour and the handling of alms. The account begins with respect received from a householder when food is given, but stresses restraint: Nirgrantha should avoid festive or vulgar entertainments, since liquor, sickness, loss of mindfulness, and improper sexual pursuit may follow. On tour, the mendicant avoids obstacles, dangerous beings, unsafe ground, and other beggars or mendicants when an alternative route exists; obtains permission before passing, clearing, entering, or leaving a secured householder’s entrance; and cleans bodily contamination only with suitable begged materials in a secluded, examined place. Food must be shared impartially. Acceptance of food and water requires scrutiny of how it was obtained, prepared, fetched, altered, or strained. Impure, unacceptable, specially procured, or inefficiently edible items, including âdhâkarmika food, sugar-cane, pea-pods, and seed-pods, must be refused. The mendicant also avoids nearer and remoter relations’ houses and accepts suitable alms given out of respect for the cloth. Finally, alms gathered for many require consultation with spiritual authorities, teacher, sub-teacher, religious guide, Sthavira, head of a Gana, Ganadhara, or founder of a Gana, and distribution as directed, without concealing or reserving better food. Inadvertent acceptance of prohibited food or salt calls for prescribed eating, disposal, permission, or sharing procedures.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Third Lesson.

A mendicant who drinks various liquors at a festive entertainment may vomit or fail to digest the food, and may suffer another disease or sickness. In drunkenness or loss of mindfulness, he might pursue a woman or a eunuch and go to her despite knowing that it should not be done.

Fifth Lesson.

A monk or nun on a begging-tour must avoid discarded meal portions, obstacles, dangerous beings, and unsafe ground when a byway exists. The monk or nun must obtain permission before clearing a householder's secured entrance, must clean a soiled body with suitable materials in a secluded place, and must share food impartially with other beggars.

Seventh Lesson.

A monk or nun on a begging-tour should consider how food or water was placed, prepared, fetched, or altered before accepting it. They must reject impure or unacceptable offerings and may accept certain water after the stated changes and straining.

Ninth Lesson.

A mendicant should not accept âdhâkarmika food, including food procured or prepared for his sake by relations or others. He should avoid the houses of his nearer or remoter relations when begging, stay apart, and later accept only suitable alms given out of respect for his cloth.

Tenth Lesson.

A mendicant who collects alms for many must consult a teacher, sub-teacher, religious guide, Sthavira, head of a Gana, Ganadhara, or founder of a Gana before distributing them, and must give the portion directed. He must not hide agreeable food under distasteful food, eat the good portion while bringing the inferior portion, or accept certain impure and unacceptable items. If he inadvertently accepts prohibited food or salt, he must follow the prescribed procedures for eating, disposal, obtaining permission, or sharing.

Sramanas in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are mendicants governed by vows of renunciation and detailed rules of conduct. They own no house, property, sons, or cattle; eat what others give; and renounce accepting anything not given. They may receive food prepared for many persons and lodging alongside guests, paupers, beggars, and Brâhmanas, but must not take an unfair share, obstruct another Sramana, or appropriate a traveller's hall without permission. Their begging is regulated: they should obtain acceptable alms from other families, avoid doubtful food, and reject food from certain households. They must not seek festivals, entertainments, or pleasing sights such as colours and forms. They travel, enter households, and undertake religious practice with a complete outfit except in unsuitable weather or insect conditions. To avoid occasions of sin, a mendicant may choose one of four rules for religious postures and stand accordingly. Thus the records move from conduct in food, travel, and lodging to strict renunciation, possession, religious posture, and avoidance of sensory pleasure.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Third Lesson.

Monks should not resolve to attend festivals preceded or followed by feasts, or go to a village for the sake of an entertainment. They should obtain acceptable alms from other families and eat them, avoid doubtful food, wear the complete outfit in specified circumstances, and reject food from the houses of Kshatriyas, kings, messengers, and relations of kings.

Fifth Lesson.

A Sramana may already be present at a householder's abode or may receive food given for all of them. A monk or nun must not stand in a Sramana's sight, overtake one to address the householder first, or take more than an impartial share of food.

Second Lecture, Called Begging for a Couch. / First Lesson. .. Third Lesson.

Sramanas and Brâhmanas are among the other guests, paupers, and beggars for whom householders may provide lodging. A mendicant should not live in a lodging-place frequented by other sectarians or in one prepared for many such religious persons when the relevant conditions make it sinful.

Seventh Lecture, Called Regulation of Possession. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

A Sramana vows to own no house, property, sons, or cattle, to eat what others give, and to accept nothing that has not been given. A Sramana must not take or appropriate property without permission and inspection.

Second Part. / The Seven Lectures. / Eighth Lecture.

A mendicant avoiding occasions to sin may choose one of four rules for performing religious postures. The mendicant then stands according to the chosen rule.

Twelfth Lecture.

A monk should not resolve to go where he will see various colours or forms merely to please the eye. These colours or forms may appear in wreaths, dressed images, dolls, clothes, woodwork, plastering, paintings, jewelry, ivory-work, strings, or leaf-cutting.

Gina in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Gina first appears

Abstract written by a model

Gina, an infinite knowing one who illumines three worlds, is the source of commandments guiding mendicants to exert themselves and respect one another, and proclaimed great vows throughout the earth.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Eleventh Lesson.

The Gina is identified as the source of the commandment according to which mendicants following different rules all exert themselves. Mendicants are instructed to respect one another accordingly.

Fourth Part. / Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Liberation.

The infinite Gina, the knowing one, proclaimed the great vows in all quarters of the earth as light illuminating the three worlds.

Claims worded by a model

  1. “as light, illumining the three worlds, (repels) darkness.”Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra) · Fourth Part. / Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Liberation.

monk in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where monk first appears

Abstract written by a model

A monk is a mendicant and fellow-ascetic governed by detailed disciplines of speech, possessions, movement, study, religious practice, and bodily conduct. Most importantly, the monk must avoid harmful conduct and places containing eggs or living beings. Speech must be true and accurate when not sinful or blamable, precise, moderate, restrained, and free of wrath, pride, deceit, greed, roughness, division, or harm. The monk may obtain limited clothing and, if youthful, strong, healthy, and well-set, wear one robe; clothes must be requested directly, worn as received, and protected. The monk may carry one inspected bowl, travel no farther than half a Yogana for it, clean it only when dry, carry it for alms, and protect it in heavy rain. For religious postures and study, the monk enters a village or suitable town and accepts only places free from eggs or living beings. As a fellow-ascetic, the monk uses a broom, avoids easing nature on living, prepared, harmful, public, sacred, cultivated, or inhabited places, and instead uses a chamber-pot in a secluded, life-free place, leaving excrement on ashes in a secluded spot.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Fourth Lecture, Called Modes of Speech. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

A monk should avoid speech spoken in wrath, pride, deception, or gain, as well as sinful, blamable, rough, stinging, coarse, divisive, or harmful speech. He should speak precisely, with moderation and restraint, using accurate or non-truth-or-untruth speech when it is not sinful.

Fifth Lecture, Called Begging of Clothes. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

A monk may beg for specified kinds of cloth and, if youthful, young, strong, healthy, and well-set, may wear one robe rather than two. He must inspect, accept, wear, maintain, air, and protect clothes according to the stated rules, and must not accept impure or unsuitable clothes.

Sixth Lecture, Called Begging for a Bowl. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

A monk may carry one bowl, but not two, and should not resolve to go farther than half a Yogana to obtain one. He may beg for a bowl made of bottle-gourd, wood, or clay, or for a used or unwanted bowl, but must refuse expensive, food-filled, or otherwise impure bowls. He must inspect and clean the bowl appropriately, carry it when entering or leaving a householder's abode, and protect it from rain.

Second Part. / The Seven Lectures. / Eighth Lecture.

A monk who wishes to perform religious postures should enter a village or a scot-free town. The monk should not accept a place infected by eggs or living beings.

Ninth Lecture.

A monk wishing to go to a place for study should not accept one infected by eggs or living beings. He may accept a place free from eggs or living beings.

Tenth Lecture.

A monk should beg for a fellow-ascetic's broom if he lacks his own. He should inspect and clean a suitable ground, avoid places containing life or associated with harm, and ease nature with a chamber-pot in a secluded place.

Claims worded by a model

Arhat in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Arhat first appears

Abstract written by a model

teach four kinds of speech [past, present, and future Arhats] All past, present, and future Arhats have taught and declared, teach and declare, and will teach and declare the four kinds of speech. They have also explained things possessing colour, smell, taste, touch, and various qualities.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fourth Lecture, Called Modes of Speech. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

All past, present, and future Arhats have taught and declared, teach and declare, and will teach and declare the four kinds of speech. They have also explained things possessing colour, smell, taste, touch, and various qualities.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra is a Gñâtri Kshatriya of Kâsyapa gotra, born in Kundapura after descending from heaven and being transferred from Devânandâ’s womb to Trisalâ’s. Son of Siddhartha and Trisalâ, he was named Vardhamâna, lived thirty years as a householder, with Yasodâ, Anoggâ, and Seshavatî named among his family. He renounced the world, practiced asceticism for twelve years, gained Manahparyâya knowledge, and attained Kevala outside Grimbhikagrâma. As an Arhat and Kevalin, omniscient and endowed with highest knowledge and intuition, he first taught gods and then men, including the five great vows, six classes of lives, and fivefold dominion.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Seventh Lecture, Called Regulation of Possession. / First Lesson. .. Second Lesson.

Mahâvîra is identified as the venerable teacher whose explanation states that dominion is fivefold.

Third Part. / Fifteenth Lecture, Called the Clauses.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra descended from heaven, was conceived in Devânandâ’s womb, transferred to Trisalâ’s womb, and was born in Kundapura. He lived thirty years among householders, renounced the world, practiced asceticism for twelve years, attained Manahparyâya knowledge, and reached Kevala outside Grimbhikagrâma.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra endowed with the highest knowledge and intuition

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra taught the five great vows, with their clauses, to the Sramanas and Nirgranthas, including Gautama. He is described as endowed with the highest knowledge and intuition.

monk in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where monk first appears

Abstract written by a model

The monk is a male mendicant whose life is organized around disciplined begging-tours and strict avoidance of improper food, conduct, and desire. He undertakes and participates in begging-tours, entering a householder’s abode for alms only after food has been prepared and distributed. He accepts food and related offerings from unblamed, uncensured families and at the proper time, including after others at an entertainment have received their share, but refuses offerings made for large communal entertainments when the conditions are improper. He does not travel, enter or leave an abode, wander, or exchange food with a heretic, householder, or a monk who does not avoid forbidden food; he also does not give food to a heretic or householder during a begging-tour. He should not seek food or drink in always liberal, always open houses, nor resolve to attend festive entertainment for food. Attendance at entertainment and entry into a householder’s abode depend on suitable conditions, such as an uncrowded gathering and a route with few living beings, seeds, and obstacles. On tour, he avoids hungry-animal gatherings when a byway exists, avoids improper gestures, speech, posture, and solicitation, and rejects food handled or prepared in prohibited ways. His dietary discipline excludes impure liquor, raw or insufficiently modified food, plants, fruits, grains, dregs, honey, ghee, garlic, specified plants and cooked items, and substances containing or fostering living beings. He must also avoid smelling food, drink, and sweet scents with eagerness, desire, greed, or covetousness. Overall, the records present a mendicant governed by careful alms-taking, nonassociation with improper companions, and extensive rules protecting purity and restraint.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra), Book II, translated by Hermann Jacobi / Second Book / First Part. / First Lecture, Call

A monk should avoid impure or unacceptable food and may accept herbs, grains, and flour only when they have been sufficiently processed. He should not enter, leave, wander, or exchange food with a heretic, householder, or a monk who does not avoid forbidden food.

Second Lesson.

The monk or nun may accept food from unblamed families and may accept food offered after others at an entertainment have received their due share. The monk or nun should not accept food prepared for large communal occasions when it is impure, and should not go to a festive entertainment for the sake of obtaining food.

Fourth Lesson.

A monk should not attend certain entertainments or enter a householder’s abode when the specified conditions make doing so improper. He may attend or enter when the conditions are suitable, and may accept acceptable alms at the proper time.

Sixth Lesson.

A monk on a begging-tour should avoid gatherings of hungry animals when a byway is available and should not lean against or point toward parts of a householder’s abode. He should not use gestures, praise, or coarse language to solicit food, and should reject food given from certain soiled, wet, or treated hands or prepared over fire.

Eighth Lesson.

A monk or nun on a begging-tour should reject impure liquor, raw or unmodified plants and foods, substances containing living beings, and various other unacceptable items. The monk or nun should also avoid smelling pleasant food, drink, or sweet scents with eagerness, desire, greed, or covetousness.

Nirgranthas in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Nirgranthas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Nirgranthas are Houseless when well provided with the five great vows and their twenty-five clauses. Instructed by the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra, they correctly practise the vows, beginning with abstinence from killing living beings. Their discipline unfolds through careful walking, examination of mind and speech, and deliberation in speaking, begging, handling utensils, and consuming inspected food and drink with permission. They renounce anger, greed, fear, mirth, sexual pleasures, and excessive or stimulating food and drink; limit and renew possession of ground; avoid discussion, contemplation, and recollection concerning women; and do not occupy beds or couches affected by desire. Finally, they avoid love and hate in response to sounds.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra endowed with the highest knowledge and intuition

Nirgranthas are instructed to practise the five great vows through twenty-five clauses. They must regulate their walking, thoughts, speech, begging, food and drink, sensual conduct, possessions, and responses to sensory experiences.

mendicant (the wise sage) in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where mendicant (the wise sage) first appears

Abstract written by a model

The mendicant is a matchless, great sage and good Sramana. Self-controlled, unattached, and free from desire, he leaves family bonds, acts, and attachments, collects alms, and lives among householders and monks. He endures words, blows, pain, and pleasure without harming any beings. Steadfast suffering increases his austerity, wisdom, and glory; with conquered sensuality he meditates on virtue, walks in wisdom and restraint, and is freed from former sins and the bed of pain.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fourth Part. / Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Liberation.

The mendicant lives self-controlled, unattached, and free from desire, collects alms, and endures insults, blows, pain, and pleasure without harming movable or immovable beings. Through wisdom, restraint, austerity, and conquered sensuality, the mendicant is freed from former sins and from the bed of pain.

monk in Akaranga Sutra (Âkârâṅga Sûtra)

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where monk first appears

Abstract written by a model

A monk is an ascetic and mendicant whose life is governed by shared rules of conduct, careful begging, nonattachment, and circumspect wandering. He lives in villages or wanders from village to village, sometimes being directed with fellow mendicants to outlying villages to beg alms. Though he may have kinsmen or relations, he does not enter their houses for food or drink, does not seek provisions before begging, and accepts alms only at the proper time and when respectfully given. He possesses an alms-bowl, eats what he receives without selecting by smell, rejects food prepared specifically for him, and consults fellow ascetics before refusing excess food. Begging food and drink follows seven rules, while different compliant rules are to be respected as efforts under the commandment of the Gina. His lodging, couch, posture, rest, and study require inspection, cleaning, and fitness: places must contain few eggs or living beings, and couches must be safe, movable, and well tied. He avoids householders' quarters, improper lodgings, and prolonged stays in halting-places, while asking about ownership before seeking lodging. During travel and the rainy season, he proceeds carefully through villages, roads, and water, avoiding injury to living beings, unsuitable aid, and involvement in householders' activities. When threatened or disturbed, he practices contemplation. Finally, he avoids going to walls or ditches to hear sounds and does not seek, like, or become enraptured with sounds of either world.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Fourth Lesson.

Mendicants following the same rules may advise one another to go to outlying villages to beg alms. They should not visit related families before begging to obtain food or other provisions, but may later enter with other mendicants and accept respectful alms.

Ninth Lesson.

A monk should not enter or leave the houses of his relations for food or drink, and should not address householders quickly when meat, fish, oil cakes, or food for a sick person is being prepared. He should consume all received food and drink without selecting parts and consult fellow-ascetics before rejecting excess.

Eleventh Lesson.

Mendicants may receive food or drink under seven prescribed rules for begging food and seven corresponding rules for begging drink. They should not claim that another mendicant has chosen the wrong rule, but should respect those who follow different rules as all exerting themselves according to the commandment of the Gina.

Second Lecture, Called Begging for a Couch. / First Lesson. .. Third Lesson.

A monk or nun must inspect and clean lodging-places and couches, avoid lodgings prepared or altered specifically for mendicants, and avoid householders' living quarters. The monk or nun must move, sleep, breathe, beg, and ease nature circumspectly so as not to injure living beings or become involved in householders' activities.

Third Lecture, Called Walking. / First Lesson. .. Third Lesson.

He should remain during the rainy season in a suitable village or scot-free town and should travel only when roads and conditions are appropriate. While wandering from village to village or crossing water, he should proceed circumspectly, avoid harming living beings, refuse unsuitable assistance or tasks, and collect himself for contemplation when threatened or disturbed.

Eleventh Lecture.

A monk should not resolve to go to places to hear drums, stringed instruments, kettledrums, wind instruments, or other sounds. He should not seek or love sounds, or become enraptured with sounds of this or the other world, whether heard or unheard, seen or unseen.

Mahâvîra in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mahâvîra is a Gina, Arhat, Sramana, Nirgrantha, great sage, and famous native of Vaisâlî, also called Gñâtriputra and Vardhamâna. He possesses highest and infinite knowledge and faith, infinite perception, omniscience, virtue, and freedom from impurity, passion, greed, and all ties. Homeless and renouncing everything, he practises control, contemplation, abstinence from women and night eating, and austerities; he keeps nothing, bears everything, and grants protection to all. He understands every philosophical system and knows this world and that beyond. By conquering wrath, pride, deceit, and greed, committing no wrong, and annihilating all Karman, he reaches insurpassable perfection. He teaches, puts forward, and proclaims the highest Law of the Ginas and sacred texts. His doctrine presents possession, violence, and attachment as causes of bondage and misery, teaches innumerable births, and makes non-killing the legitimate conclusion of wisdom. In his concluding teaching, monks are urged to avoid killing, abstain from sins, and seek spiritual welfare. He is praised as the flag and best of monks, the best teacher of Nirvâna, the wisest sage and man, and without an equal.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I / First Book. / First Lecture, Called the Doctrine. / First Chapter. .. Fourth Chapter.

Mahâvîra teaches that possession, violence, and attachment cause bondage and misery. He teaches that beings undergo births without number and that non-killing is the legitimate conclusion of wisdom.

Second Lecture, Called the Destruction of Karman. / First Chapter. .. Third Chapter.

Arhat Gñâtriputra speaks the concluding teaching, declaring that monks should avoid killing living beings, abstain from sins, and seek spiritual welfare. He is described as possessing the highest knowledge and faith and as a famous native of Vaisâlî.

Sixth Lecture, Called Praise of Mahâvîra.

Mahâvîra, also called Gñâtriputra and Vardhamâna, is described as omniscient, infinitely perceptive, virtuous, free from passion, and without impurity. He proclaimed the highest Law, conquered wrath, pride, deceit, and greed, annihilated all Karman, and reached insurpassable perfection. He is presented as the wisest of sages and the best of those who taught Nirvâna.

Ninth Lecture, Called the Law.

Mahâvîra is called the wise Brâhmana, the Nirgrantha, and the great sage. He possesses infinite knowledge and faith and teaches the Law and the sacred texts.

sage (Mahâvîra's interlocutor) in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where sage (Mahâvîra's interlocutor) first appears

Abstract written by a model

A Leader, Saviour, and Venerable One who knows past, present, and future, removes obstacles to right faith, and incomparably knows and expounds the Law. Not inclined to heretical doctrines, he affirms his teaching as true and explains the names Brâhmana, Sramana, Bhikshu, and Nirgrantha.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Fifteenth Lecture, Called the Yamakas.

The Leader and Saviour knows what is past, present, and to come, and annihilates the hindrances to right faith. He knows and expounds the incomparable Law and is not inclined towards heretical doctrines.

Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Song.

The Venerable One explains the reasons for the names Brâhmana, Sramana, Bhikshu, and Nirgrantha. He declares the teaching true because he is the Saviour.

Sramanas in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are religious ascetics, not monks who seek houses with fine food. They may hold true or heretical doctrines; those who deny the true Law, pursue pleasures, or abuse the pure path enter a wrong path. Heretical Sramanas are ignorant and careless, incur sin, misery, destruction, and great danger, and remain subject to Karman, death, and repeated birth.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I / First Book. / First Lecture, Called the Doctrine. / First Chapter. .. Fourth Chapter.

Sramanas are described as holding both true and heretical doctrines. Those who reject the true Law, indulge in pleasures, or follow wrong views remain subject to Karman, death, and repeated birth.

Eleventh Lecture, Called the Path.

Heretical Sramanas are described as ignorant, careless, and unworthy when they pursue pleasures or enter a wrong path. Like birds meditating on catching fish or a blind-born man in a leaky boat, they incur sin, misery, destruction, and great danger.

Claims worded by a model

3 claims from 3 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

Sramanas ignores and denies these true words (some men)open the passage →
  1. “Some men, Sramanas and Brâhmanas, who ignore and deny these true words, adhere (to their own tenets), and are given to pleasures. (6)”Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I · Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I / First Book. / First Lecture, Called the Doctrine. / First Chapter. .. Fourth Chapter.
Sramanas pursue pleasures (some heretical, unworthy Sramanas) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “As dhaṅkas, herons, ospreys, cormorants, and pheasants meditate upon capturing fish, (which is) a sinful and very low meditation, so some heretical, unworthy Sramanas contemplate the pursuit of pleasures; (they are) sinful and very low like herons. (27, 28)”Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I · Eleventh Lecture, Called the Path.
Sramanas incur great danger (by entering the full current of Samsâra) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “As a blind-born man getting into a leaky boat wants to reach the shore, but is drowned during the passage; so some unworthy, heretical Sramanas, having got into the full current (of the Samsâra), will incur great danger. (30, 31)”Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I · Eleventh Lecture, Called the Path.

Great Hero in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Great Hero first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Great Hero is a being without Karman who understands Karman, is not born again or dies, annihilates former works, and creates no new works.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fifteenth Lecture, Called the Yamakas.

The Great Hero understands Karman, has no Karman, and does not die. He annihilates former works, does not do new ones, and abstains from works entailed by birth.

Nirgrantha in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha first appears

Abstract written by a model

A Nirgrantha is a religious ascetic: single, awakened, disciplined, and knowing the absolute âtman and true Self. Possessing Samitis and equanimity, it renounces sin and worldly honour, seeks and knows the Law, subdues senses, strives for Liberation, and abandons the body.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Song.

A Nirgrantha is single, knowing the absolute âtman, awakened, proof against sins, and well disciplined. A Nirgrantha possesses the Samitis and equanimity, knows the true nature of the Self, renounces the causes of sin, seeks the Law and Liberation, and lives restrained.

Kâsyapa in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Kâsyapa first appears

Abstract written by a model

Kâsyapa is an illustrious, omniscient religious teacher associated with and proclaiming a Law practiced through abstinence, control, hardship, and care for sick brothers. This Law is followed by virtuous Ginas of past and future. Kâsyapa warns that attachment is a dangerous whirlpool avoided by the wise but entered by the ignorant, describes hellish pains and punishments to Kêvalin, and proclaims the very difficult Path by which a monk crosses the dreadful current of Samsâra toward the supreme condition.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lecture, Called the Destruction of Karman. / First Chapter. .. Third Chapter.

Kâsyapa is associated with the Law followed by those who abstain from sensual pleasures and practice control. The Law of Kâsyapa is also said to be followed by virtuous Ginas of the past and future.

Third Lecture, Called the Knowledge of Troubles / First Chapter. .. Fourth Chapter.

Kâsyapa is said to have pointed out whirlpools that the wise avoid and the ignorant enter. The Law proclaimed by Kâsyapa instructs monks to endure hardships and care for sick brothers.

Fifth Lecture, Called Description of the Hells. / First Chapter. .. Second Chapter.

Kâsyapa, described as illustrious and omniscient, answers Kêvalin by describing the pains and punishments suffered in the hells.

Eleventh Lecture, Called the Path.

Kâsyapa proclaimed the difficult Path and the Law by which a monk crosses the dreadful current of Samsâra. The speaker has learned this Path and explains it in due order.

Sramanas in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are houseless religious ascetics and Kriyâvâdins who beg according to the Law, observe conduct rules, perform austerities, control their senses, and seek final liberation for the soul. They give up domestic ties, worldly desires, passions, property, killing, lies, and sexual intercourse; avoid women and sexual pleasures; and endure calamities without yielding to sin. They understand the world according to their lights and teach that misery arises from one’s own works. Those who fear want may turn to worldly sciences, but are instructed to abandon household ties and wander for the soul’s welfare. A true Sramana is detached, unhampered by obstacles, free from desires, and ultimately abandons the body. When free from attachment and preaching rightly, the Sramana is a Nirgrantha who teaches monastic conduct.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lecture, Called the Destruction of Karman. / First Chapter. .. Third Chapter.

Sramanas are instructed to observe the rules of conduct, perform austerities, control their senses, and remain detached from domestic ties. A worthy Sramana should endure calamities without giving way to passions.

Third Lecture, Called the Knowledge of Troubles / First Chapter. .. Fourth Chapter.

Some Sramanas, knowing themselves to be weak, seek worldly sciences when they fear want. They are instructed to abandon ties to the house and wander for the welfare of the soul.

Fourth Lecture, Called Knowledge of Women. / First Chapter. .. Second Chapter.

A Sramana is expected to avoid women and sexual pleasures, even when women offer robes, alms, food, or drink. One who secretly commits sins while speaking holy words is regarded by the wise as a deceiver and rogue.

Twelfth Lecture, Called the Creed.

They understand the world according to their lights and teach that misery is produced by one's own works, not by those of somebody else.

Fourteenth Lecture, Called the Nirgrantha.

The Nirgrantha teaches the conduct required of monks who have given up worldly ties and follow the creed.

Sixteenth Lecture, Called the Song.

A Sramana is not hampered by obstacles and is free from desires, property, killing, telling lies, sexual intercourse, and passions such as wrath, pride, deceit, greed, love, and hate. A Sramana subdues the senses, is well qualified, and abandons the body.

Ginas in Sutrakritanga (Sûtrakritâṅga), Book I

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Ginas first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Ginas, past and future, commend virtuous conduct under the Law of Kâsyapa. They teach control, nonviolence, austerities, carefulness, and the path to perfection. Associated with the noble Law for monks, their words awaken novices to know it; their doctrine is heard, treasured, and faithfully delivered.

Ninth Lecture, Called the Law.

The Ginas are associated with the noble Law that a monk should follow.

Fourteenth Lecture, Called the Nirgrantha.

The Ginas provide the words through which a novice comes to know the Law after being awakened. Their doctrine is to be heard, treasured, and faithfully delivered.

followers of the Sramanas in Sutrakritanga, Book II

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where followers of the Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Followers of the Sramanas understand living and nonliving things, doctrine including Âsravas and Samvara, virtues, sins, Karman, bondage, and liberation. They practise vows, fasts, and self-purification, support Nirgrantha Sramanas, and confess sins. Although some cannot become monastics, they gradually conform through Pôsaha, limits, or fasting for death, and after perfection are reborn as gods.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Now the explanation of the second subject, viz. merit, is as follows:

Followers of the Sramanas understand living and nonliving things, virtues and sins, Karman, bondage, and final liberation. They observe religious vows and fasts, support Nirgrantha Sramanas, and are reborn as gods after confessing their sins and dying.

Seventh Lecture, Called Nâlandâ.

Sramanas are described as renouncing injury to living beings and as receiving instruction in vows, discipline, and religious conduct. Some followers cannot enter the monastic state but gradually conform to the community, observe Pôsaha practices, set limits, or prepare for death by fasting.

Nirgrantha Sramanas in Sutrakritanga, Book II

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Nirgrantha Sramanas are a group with a creed accepted by their followers, who provide food, clothing, medicines, and necessities. They are described as teaching or debating renunciation of injury to living beings; Gautama criticizes a teaching that renounces injury to movable beings while permitting it to immovable beings as noxious, misleading laymen, and voiding renunciation.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Now the explanation of the second subject, viz. merit, is as follows:

Nirgranthas have a creed that followers of the Sramanas regard as true, while other creeds are considered futile. Nirgrantha Sramanas receive food, clothing, medicines, and other necessities from those followers.

Seventh Lecture, Called Nâlandâ.

Nirgrantha Sramanas are described as teaching or debating rules about renunciation of injury to living beings. Gautama says that those who teach that injury to movable beings may be renounced while injury to immovable beings remains permitted speak noxious speech and mislead laymen.

Mahâvîra in Sutrakritanga, Book II

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mahâvîra is a Venerable Ascetic, Sramana, and teacher, also called Gñâtriputra. He gives the lotus-pool simile and explains it to Sramanas, discusses four doctrines of heretical teachers, and teaches discourses on Activity, Knowledge of Food, and renunciation. His teaching describes acquiring Karman, sins connected with mind, speech, and body, the six classes of living beings, and the controlled monk’s avoidance and renunciation of sins. Though single and alone amid many followers, he moves from wandering as one monk to teaching many monks. He avoids wrong opinions, acquires no new Karman, annihilates old Karman, and teaches the vow leading to Môksha. At Nâlandâ, he receives Udaka into the creed of five great vows and Pratikramana.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Sutrakritanga, Book II / First Lecture, Called the Lotus.

Mahâvîra gives the lotus-pool simile, explains its meanings to the Sramanas, and discusses four doctrines held by heretical teachers.

Second Lecture, Called on Activity.

Mahâvîra is the Venerable teacher from whom Sudharman heard the discourse on Activity. He is presented as having declared the thirteen kinds of acquiring Karman, together with all Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future.

Third Lecture, Called Knowledge of Food.

Mahâvîra is the Venerable teacher from whom Sudharman heard the discourse.

Fourth Lecture, Called Renunciation of Activity.

Mahâvîra teaches that the Self may commit sins even when it does not consider the operations of mind, speech, and body or see even a dream. He assigns the six classes of living beings as the reason for this and teaches that a controlled monk avoids and renounces sins.

Sixth Lecture, Called .

Mahâvîra is said to have moved from wandering as a single monk to teaching many monks. He acquires no new Karman, annihilates the old, avoids wrong opinions, and teaches the vow leading to Môksha.

Seventh Lecture, Called Nâlandâ.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra receives Udaka after Gautama brings him there. In Mahâvîra’s presence, Udaka circumambulates, praises, worships him, and changes to the creed which enjoins the five great vows and the Pratikramana.

Sramanas in Sutrakritanga, Book II

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are long-lived religious practitioners whose true character is defined by disciplined, nonviolent conduct. They teach the Law, the great vows, and the five small vows; understand the Âsravas and their stoppage; exercise self-control; avoid Karman; and use patient, resigned, virtuous speech. They should avoid specially prepared food from compassion and fear of injuring living beings, and are not householders who use cold water, eat seeds, or have intercourse with women. The records also warn that some Sramanas kill living beings or acquire property and may relapse into such habits after becoming monks. They may be robbed, insulted, or denied alms. In the Lotus lecture, they hear a simile, ask Mahâvîra for its meaning, and receive doctrinal teaching. The account contrasts teachers of violence, destined for suffering, with those who reject violence.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

Sutrakritanga, Book II / First Lecture, Called the Lotus.

The Sramanas hear the simile, ask Mahâvîra to explain its meaning, and receive his interpretation and doctrinal teaching.

I say: here in the East, West, North, and South there are some men, viz. Âryas,

Some Sramanas are described as killers of living beings and acquirers of property. If they retain these habits, they may relapse into them after becoming monks.

Second Lecture, Called on Activity.

Sramanas are among those whom men may rob, insult, or refuse alms. The text also presents religious practitioners as needing to understand the thirteen kinds of committing sins and describes proper monastic conduct.

Now the explanation of the second subject, viz. merit, is as follows:

Sramanas are presented both as objects of support from followers of the Sramanas and as teachers whose doctrines concern the treatment of living beings. Those who teach violence are destined for suffering, while those who reject violence avoid such suffering.

Sixth Lecture, Called .

Sramanas are defined by teaching the great vows and the five small vows, understanding the Âsravas and their stoppage, exercising control, and avoiding Karman. Those who use cold water, eat seeds, or have intercourse with women are said to be householders rather than Sramanas.

Abstract written by a model

The Arhats and Bhagavats of the past, present, and future declare that all sorts of living beings should not be slain, treated with violence, abused, tormented, or driven away.

monk in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where monk first appears

Abstract written by a model

A monk is a houseless, poor wandering mendicant who has discarded worldly ties, property, former connections, attachment, hatred, pleasures, and desire for women. The central demand is disciplined self-control: he keeps the five vows, avoids untruth, sinful speech, deceit, killing, and injury in thought, word, and act; accepts only suitable alms for sustaining life; eats without daintiness; and follows the Law of ascetics strictly. He remains watchful and careful, guards his senses and celibacy, meditates, studies with zeal, acknowledges his teacher, and preaches the road of peace. His path requires enduring, learning, and conquering the twenty-two troubles without anger, attachment, forbidden conduct, or despair, while wandering without a fixed residence and bearing hardships. Human birth, hearing and believing the Law, and strenuous fulfillment of it lead him to restrain himself and shake off sinfulness. Outward ascetic marks alone do not ensure morality: an ignorant, egoistical, greedy, undisciplined, or loose-speaking monk is ill-behaved and may not reach Nirvâna. In contrast, humility, truthfulness, kindness, learning, austerities, and restraint make him worthy of instruction, superior to other monks, deep, fearless, and difficult to overcome. By conquering the will, casting aside fetters, annihilating Karman, and attaining self-discipline, Samvara, and contemplation, he reaches the highest place, liberation, or may become Siddha; restrained monks may also become gods of great power.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

10 sections; each heading opens the text

Uttaradhyayana Sutra / First Lecture.

The houseless monk has got rid of all worldly ties and is instructed to follow disciplined conduct. He should behave properly toward his teacher, collect alms according to sanctioned rules, eat moderately, meditate, and keep the five vows.

Second Lecture.

The monk is instructed to endure the twenty-two troubles without anger, attachment, forbidden conduct, or despair. He should wander without fixed residence or property, bear bodily and social hardships, practice self-control and austerities, and continue searching for the welfare of his soul.

Third Lecture.

An ascetic who has obtained human birth, heard and believed in the Law, and fulfills it strenuously should restrain himself and shake off sinfulness.

Fourth Lecture.

A monk should walk and live carefully, remain watchful, control himself, avoid hatred toward external things, and remove delusion, pride, deceit, and greed. By conquering the will and guarding himself, a monk can quickly obtain liberation.

Fifth Lecture.

Monks do not all possess good morality, and outward marks such as bark, skin, nakedness, twisted hair, or baldness do not save a sinful ascetic. Restrained monks become either free from all misery or gods of great power.

Eighth Lecture.

A monk should abandon former connections, attachment, hatred, pleasures, and desire for women. He should carefully accept suitable alms only for sustaining life, avoid injuring beings, and strictly keep the Law.

Tenth Lecture.

The enlightened and liberated monk should control himself in a village or a town. He should preach to all the road of peace.

Eleventh Lecture.

A monk who is ignorant, egoistical, greedy, undisciplined, or loose in speech is ill-behaved, while one who is humble, steady, truthful, receptive to advice, and kind is well-behaved. A very learned monk is praised as superior to others and, after his Karman is annihilated, monks who are deep, fearless, difficult to overcome, learned, and self-controlled go to the highest place.

Sixteenth Lecture.

Monks who hear and understand the ten conditions are expected to attain greater self-discipline, Samvara, contemplation, sense-guarding, chastity, and attentiveness to religious duties. A chaste monk should live detached, eat in the prescribed manner, avoid sensual pleasures, and remain steadfast in the Law.

Eighteenth Lecture.

Monks are expected to practise self-control, sacred study, austerities, and right conduct. They should abandon heretical doctrines, personal fancies, and deeds productive of evil.

Nirgrantha in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha first appears

Abstract written by a model

Nirgrantha is a monk defined by disciplined renunciation. He adopts the Law to live with fellow-monks, abandons former friends, desires, love, delusion, house, and ties, and wanders as an unknown beggar. Upright, righteous, sinless, versed in sacred lore, and devoted to protecting his soul, he controls thoughts, words, acts, and senses; keeps vows; practises austerities and self-discipline; meditates on the soul; endures heat, cold, and lowly lodging; and expects neither hospitality nor praise. He avoids sins, harm, worldly arts, divination, medical treatment, gain from householders, and praise of social groups or artists. Content with little food and even unappealing fare, he is not angry when offerings are withheld. The account then gives specific safeguards for solitary chastity and freedom from sensual attachment: he avoids unsuitable resting places, women, cattle, and eunuchs; does not converse, sit, observe, listen to, or recall pleasures connected with women; and rejects rich food, excess, ornaments, and pleasant sense objects. These precautions preserve chastity, rules, health, and faith.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Fifteenth Lecture.

The monk adopts the Law, lives uprightly with other monks, abandons former connections and desires, and wanders as an unknown beggar. He controls himself, keeps the vows, practises austerities, protects his soul from sins, endures hardships, avoids worldly arts and gain, and lives single without a house or friends.

Sixteenth Lecture.

A Nirgrantha is instructed not to rest in places frequented by women, cattle, or eunuchs, converse with women, share their seat, observe their charms, listen to their sounds behind a barrier, recall past pleasures with them, eat well-dressed food or to excess, wear ornaments, or care for pleasant sense objects. Violating these precautions may lead to doubt about chastity, sensual desire, remorse, broken rules, passion, illness, or desertion of the faith.

Mahâvîra in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

Mahâvîra is a Venerable ascetic of Kâsyapa Gôtra and a teacher. Across lectures, he declares twenty-two troubles wandering monks must endure and conquer, describes death against one’s will as an ignorant pleasure-attached man’s cruel end, and teaches conditions for preserving chastity and fulfilling religious duties.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Second Lecture.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra declares twenty-two troubles that a monk must learn and know, bear and conquer in order not to be vanquished while living as a wandering mendicant.

Fifth Lecture.

Mahâvîra describes the death against one's will as the death of an ignorant man attached to pleasures who does cruel actions.

Sixteenth Lecture.

Mahâvîra is the Venerable teacher whose Discourse Sudharman recounts. His teaching concerns the conditions by which monks preserve chastity and fulfil their religious duties.

Claims worded by a model

4 claims from 4 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

Mahâvîra belongs to Kâsyapa Gôtra (as an ascetic) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “Here, forsooth, the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra of the Kâsyapa Gôtra has declared twenty-two troubles which a monk must learn and know, bear and conquer, in order not to be vanquished by them when he lives the life of a wandering mendicant.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Second Lecture.
Mahâvîra declared twenty-two troubles (for monks living the wandering mendicant life) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “Here, forsooth, the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra of the Kâsyapa Gôtra has declared twenty-two troubles which a monk must learn and know, bear and conquer, in order not to be vanquished by them when he lives the life of a wandering mendicant.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Second Lecture.
Mahâvîra describes death against one's will (as the first kind of death) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “Mahâvîra has (thus) described the first kind in which an ignorant man, being attached to pleasures, does very cruel actions.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Fifth Lecture.
