Nirgrantha in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha first appears

Abstract written by a model

A Nirgrantha enters an order, learns Law, receives discipline, and attains difficult Bôdhi, but may afterward live as he likes and become a bad Sramana.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Seventeenth Lecture.

A Nirgrantha may enter the order, learn the Law, receive religious discipline, and obtain Bôdhi which is difficult to obtain. Afterwards, he may begin to live as he likes and become a bad Sramana.

Sramana in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Sramana first appears

Abstract written by a model

A Sramana repeatedly suppresses delusion and controls himself, knowing and renouncing women; though roughly affected by externals, he must not hate them inwardly.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Fourth Lecture.

A Sramana who repeatedly suppresses delusion and controls himself is affected roughly by external things, but should not hate them in his mind.

  1. “In this world men have a natural liking for women; he who knows (and renounces) them, will easily perform his duties as a Sramana.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Second Lecture.

Ginas in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

author unknown · the work’s page · read it where Ginas first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Ginas are enlightened authorities who proclaim an eternal Law, teaching souls, sin’s consequences, and righteousness; Sañgaya and many kings adopt their faith.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Eighteenth Lecture.

The Ginas are presented as the enlightened authorities whose creed teaches the existence of the soul, the consequences of sin, and the path of righteousness. Sañgaya adopted their faith, and the unit names many kings who followed it.

Vardhamâna in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Vardhamâna first appears

Abstract written by a model

Vardhamâna, a venerable Gina and Prophet of the Law, is world-known; through Gautama, his Law mandates five vows and monastic nudity.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Twenty-Third Lecture.

Vardhamâna is the Gina and prophet of the Law known throughout the world. His famous disciple is Gautama, and the Law he taught enjoins five vows and forbids clothes for a monk.

Nirgranthas in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Nirgranthas first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Nirgranthas follow doctrines centered on great vows, penance, self-control, and correct behavior; careless or sensual followers fail, while strict practice frees them from sinful influences and crosses Samsâra.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

Twentieth Lecture.

The Nirgranthas are described as following a road requiring strict vows, penance, self-control, and freedom from sinful influences. Those who adopt their Law but act carelessly or pursue sensuality will suffer and fail to attain liberation.

Twenty-Sixth Lecture.

The Nirgranthas have crossed the ocean of Samsâra by practising correct behaviour.

Claims worded by a model

3 claims from 3 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

Nirgranthas have great vows (as their defining discipline) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “Thus the austere and calm, great ascetic and great sage who kept great vows and possessed great fame, preached at great length this great sermon: the great duty of the Nirgranthas. (53)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Twentieth Lecture.
Nirgranthas have doctrines consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “As a Srâvaka he was well versed in the doctrines of the Nirgranthas.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Twenty-First Lecture.
Nirgranthas cross Samsâra (by practising correct behaviour) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “I shall declare the correct behaviour (sâmâkârî) which causes freedom from all misery; by practising it the Nirgranthas have crossed the ocean of Samsâra. (1)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Twenty-Sixth Lecture.

Sramana in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Sramana first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramana is an ascetic, saint, and houseless monk defined by restraint, concentration, penance, self-control, virtue, and freedom from property and egoism. After giving up household life and taking Pravragyâ, he observes the five great vows and five Samitis, is protected by the three Guptis, and renounces attachments, wealth, possessions, desire, love, greed, and worldly success or failure. He protects living beings, prevents and destroys bad Karman through discipline and austerities, while recognizing that misery and happiness arise through his own self. His path requires constant exertion concerning vows, senses, conduct, duties, living beings, faults, scriptures, and other prescribed points. He seeks righteousness through study, pondering the Sûtras, serving teachers, choosing wise company, eating permitted food in proper measure, and living alone in suitable, pure, quiet places. He restrains his senses and tongue, avoids pleasures, women, trade, construction, cooking, fire, possessions, praise, and comforts; instead he lives on carefully begged alms and meditates only on true things. He bears calamities well, remains detached from pleasant and unpleasant things, and maintains chastity. At death he rejects food and drink, leaves the human body, and is liberated from misery. Through sustained austerity and freedom from Âsravas, he obtains absolute knowledge, eternal beatitude, release from Samsâra, and Nirvâna.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

Nineteenth Lecture.

A Sramana is described as practising penance, self-restraint, self-control, and virtue. Mrigâputra becomes a Sramana who is impartial, detached, and free from property and egoism.

Twentieth Lecture.

The restrained and concentrated saint sits beneath a tree at the Mandikukshi Kaitya and explains that neither relatives nor worldly possessions can protect a person from suffering. He recounts his illness, ordination, and teaching on the great duty of the Nirgranthas.

Thirtieth Lecture.

A monk becomes free from Âsravas by abstaining from destroying life, lying, taking what is not given, sexual indulgence, property, and eating at night. By performing external and internal austerities, the monk can be thoroughly released from the Circle of Births.

Thirty-First Lecture.

A monk avoids love and hatred, harmful, conceited, and delusive acts, calamities, sinful meditations, and many other listed faults. He exerts himself with regard to vows, senses, duties, virtues, and other prescribed points; a clever monk who does so will soon be thoroughly released from the Circle of Births.

Thirty-Second Lecture.

A Sramana seeking righteousness should serve Gurus and old teachers, study, ponder the Sûtras, eat the proper quantity of allowed food, choose a virtuous companion, and live suitably for seclusion. He should restrain his senses, avoid sensual objects and women, and remain indifferent to pleasant and unpleasant things.

Thirty-Fifth Lecture.

The monk should abstain from killing, lying, stealing, carnal intercourse, desire, love, greed, building, cooking, lighting fires, buying, selling, and seeking praise or possessions. He should live in a pure and quiet place, beg for alms, restrain his senses and tongue, meditate on true things, and remain unattached until death.

Ginas in Uttaradhyayana Sutra

Hermann Jacobi · the work’s page · read it where Ginas first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Ginas are teachers with the highest road, abandoned by self-willed sinners pursuing pleasure. Possessing the best knowledge, they teach final deliverance, six kinds of substances, nine truths, and the Law of monks. They prescribe precepts and exertion in righteousness; following and adoring the twenty-four Ginas purifies faith. They also describe Îshatprâgbhâra as an open umbrella.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

Twentieth Lecture.

The Ginas are described as having the highest road, which the self-willed sinner leaves when pursuing pleasure. The wise person is instructed to follow their road.

Twenty-Eighth Lecture.

The Ginas possess the best knowledge and have taught the road to final deliverance, the six kinds of substances, and the nine truths.

Twenty-Ninth Lecture.

The Ginas prescribe the precepts according to which creatures practise the teachings. Adoration of the twenty-four Ginas purifies faith, and their proclaimed Law is to be practised in this world and the next.

B. Living Beings.

The best of the Jinas are said to have described Îshatprâgbhâra as resembling an open umbrella.

Claims worded by a model

2 claims from 2 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

Ginas have highest road (abandoned by the self-willed sinner) consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “"Thus the self-willed sinner who leaves the road of the highest Ginas, who with the appetite of an osprey is desirous of pleasure, will grieve in useless sorrow. (50)”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Twentieth Lecture.
Ginas teach Law of monks consolidatedopen the passage →
  1. “One should keep the five great vows, viz. not to kill, to speak the truth, not to steal, to be chaste, to have no property whatever; a wise man should follow the Law taught by the Ginas.”Uttaradhyayana Sutra · Twenty-First Lecture.

Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra in Kalpa Sutra

Bhadrabâhu · the work’s page · read it where Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra first appears

Abstract written by a model

Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra is the last Tîrthakara, of the Gñâtri Kshatriya race and Kâsyapa gotra, son of Siddhârtha and associated with the names Vardhamâna and Sramana. His life unfolds from descent from the great Vimâna Pushpottara through conception: he first takes embryonic form in Devânandâ’s womb and is transferred to Trisalâ’s womb. Brought into the Gñâtri family, he is called Vardhamâna because its wealth and prosperity increase. While still in the womb, he remains motionless to avoid troubling his mother, moves when he learns her sorrow, and resolves not to become houseless while his parents live. He is born a healthy boy in Kundapura. After thirty years as a householder, he leaves property, kingdom, and possessions, practices asceticism and meditation, and after more than twelve years below perfection attains Kevala: supreme, complete knowledge and intuition. Thereafter he is a Kevalin, Gina, and Arhat, establishes a large community with nine Ganas and eleven Ganadharas, and has Indrabhûti as oldest disciple. He commences Paggusan and proclaims Paryushanâkalpa at Râgagriha’s Kaitya Gunasilaka. After under thirty years as a Kevalin, within a lifespan of seventy-two years, he dies in Pâpâ as a Siddha, liberated and freed from pains; his final liberation is in Svâti.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

7 sections; each heading opens the text

Kalpa Sutra / Lives of the Ginas. / Life of Mahâvîra.

Mahâvîra is the Venerable Ascetic and the last of the Tîrthakaras. He descended from the great Vimâna Pushpottara, took the form of an embryo in Devânandâ's womb, and was later transferred to Trisalâ's womb.

In that night in which the embryo of the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra was removed

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra is the person whose embryo is removed from the womb of the Brâhmanî Devânandâ and transferred to that of the Kshatriyânî Trisalâ.

When the Kshatriyânî Trisalâ, having seen these fourteen illustrious, great drea

He is brought into the family of the Gñâtris, after which the family’s wealth, possessions, power, and glory increase, leading his parents to resolve to name him Vardhamâna. He remains motionless in the womb to spare his mother anxiety, then moves when he learns of her sorrow. He resolves not to enter the state of houselessness during his parents’ lives and is later born as a perfectly healthy boy.

In that night in which the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra was born, there was a divi

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra was born in Kundapura and belonged to the Kâsyapa gotra. He renounced his property and kingdom, practiced asceticism and meditation, attained Kevala, established a large community, and finally died in Pâpâ after becoming liberated.

(147) Since the time that the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvira died, &c.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvira is stated to have died. Nine centuries have elapsed since his death, and the text records the eightieth year of the tenth century, with another redaction giving the ninety-third year.

List of the Sthaviras.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra had nine Ganas and eleven Ganadharas. He was of the Kâsyapa gotra, and his lineage is traced through successive Sthaviras.

Rules for Yatis.

The Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra commenced the Paggusan when a month and twenty nights of the rainy season had elapsed. In the town of Râgagriha, in the Kaitya Gunasilaka, he proclaimed the Lecture called Paryushanâkalpa.

Sramanas in Kalpa Sutra

Bhadrabâhu · the work’s page · read it where Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are ascetics who follow the Law defended by Mahâvîra and communities associated with Rishabha. Their communities are led by named heads and range from fourteen thousand under Indrabhûti to sixteen thousand under Âryadatta for Pârsva and eighty-four thousand under Rishabhasena for Rishabha. They include sages and disciples with specified attainments. During Kshudrâtma’s stay in Mahâvîra’s natal asterism, respect and honour for Sramanas, Nirgrantha monks, and nuns decline.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

In that night in which the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra was born, there was a divi

Sramanas are described as ascetics, and the Venerable Ascetic Mahâvîra was called Sramana because he was devoid of love and hate. After the great Graha called Kshudrâtma entered his natal asterism, less respect and honour would be paid to Sramanas, Nirgrantha monks, and nuns until it departed.

Life of Pârsva.

Pârsva instituted an excellent community of sixteen thousand Sramanas with Âryadatta at their head. He also had three hundred and fifty sages who knew the fourteen Pûrvas and other groups of Sramanas with specified attainments.

Life of Rishabha.

Rishabha had an excellent community of eighty-four thousand Sramanas with Rishabhasena at their head. He also had twenty thousand male disciples who had reached perfection and twenty-two thousand nine hundred sages in their last birth.

Abstract written by a model

Sramanas are an excellent community of eighteen thousand led by Varadatta, within Arishtanemi’s wider community of sages and disciples.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Life of Arishtanemi.

The Arhat Arishtanemi had an excellent community of eighteen thousand Sramanas with Varadatta at their head. He also had four hundred sages who knew the fourteen Pûrvas, fifteen hundred Avadhi-knowing sages, fifteen hundred Kevalins, and other groups of sages and disciples.

Abstract written by a model

has_death_after Sîtala [by a krore of Sâgaropamas less 42,003 years and eight and a half months] Vîra’s death is used as the reference point for Sîtala’s death. Sîtala died a krore of Sâgaropamas less 42,003 years and eight and a half months before Vîra.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Epochs of the Intermediate Tîrthakaras.

Vîra’s death is used as the reference point for Sîtala’s death. Sîtala died a krore of Sâgaropamas less 42,003 years and eight and a half months before Vîra.

Nirgrantha Sramanas in Kalpa Sutra

Bhadrabâhu · the work’s page · read it where Nirgrantha Sramanas first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Nirgrantha Sramanas are present spiritual descendants of Ârya-Sudharman; following earlier teachers and the Sthaviras, they commence Paggusan after a month and twenty rainy-season nights.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

List of the Sthaviras.

The Nirgrantha Sramanas of the present time are all described as spiritual descendants of Ârya-Sudharman.

Rules for Yatis.

The Nirgrantha Sramanas of the present time commence the Paggusan after a month and twenty nights of the rainy season have elapsed, following the example of earlier religious teachers.

Claims worded by a model

2 claims from 2 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

  1. “The Nirgrantha Sramanas of the present time are all (spiritual) descendants of the monk Ârya Sudharman, the rest of the Ganadharas left no descendants.”Kalpa Sutra · List of the Sthaviras.
Nirgrantha Sramanas commence Paggusan (in the present time, following the Sthaviras)open the passage →
  1. “As they have done, so do the Nirgrantha Sramanas of the present time.”Kalpa Sutra · Rules for Yatis.

the Arhats in Kalpa Sutra

Bhadrabâhu · the work’s page · read it where the Arhats first appears

Abstract written by a model

The Arhats are revered in opening obeisances and Sakra’s praise; across past, present, and future, they are born or moved at conception into high, noble, royal, or pure families.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

Kalpa Sutra / Lives of the Ginas. / Life of Mahâvîra.

The Arhats are revered in the opening obeisances and in Sakra's praise. Sakra reflects that Arhats are born in high, noble, royal, or similarly pure families, not in low or brahmanical families.

Claims worded by a model

2 claims from 2 quoted facts, in order of appearance. The arrow opens the text at the passage; a claim opens to every quotation behind it.

the Arhats are born in high families (past, present, and future)open the passage →
  1. “For indeed Arhats, Kakravartins, Baladevas, and Vasudevas, in the past, present, and future, are born in high families, noble families, royal families”Kalpa Sutra · Kalpa Sutra / Lives of the Ginas. / Life of Mahâvîra.
the Arhats are removed to high families (when conceived in low families)open the passage →
  1. “to cause the Arhats and Bhagavats to be removed from such-like low, mean, &c., families, to such-like high, noble, &c., families”Kalpa Sutra · Kalpa Sutra / Lives of the Ginas. / Life of Mahâvîra.
